Nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you saw, heard, or felt things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: See or hear things? Like... like ghosts or something? No, no, I've never had anything like that happen. My mind doesn't play tricks on me. Though, you know, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins, she swears her house is haunted. Says she hears footsteps in the attic every night around 2 AM. I told her it's probably just raccoons. We get a lot of them around here, especially since they tore down that patch of woods behind the old elementary school to build those new townhouses. Ugly things, too, all gray vinyl siding. No character at all. They put them up so fast, I bet the plumbing is a nightmare. I remember when my buddy Stan bought a new build back in the nineties, and the pipes burst his first winter. Ruined his whole basement. He had this beautiful model train setup down there, O-gauge, took him years to build the little towns and mountains. Just washed it all away. It was a real shame. Stan was so heartbroken he never rebuilt it. He took up birdwatching instead. But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. No, I don't hear or see anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that other people don’t seem to understand, or felt like your thoughts aren’t making sense in a way that’s different from how other people think?\n\nPatient: Thoughts that don't make sense? No, my thoughts are perfectly clear to me. I mean, sometimes I'll try to explain how to fix a carburetor to my nephew, and he just stares at me blankly, but that's because kids these days don't know the first thing about engines. It's all computers now. You plug a little machine into the dashboard and it tells you what's wrong. Where's the skill in that? I learned by listening to the engine, you know? My uncle taught me. He had this old Chevy, a '57 Bel Air, cherry red. Beautiful car. We used to spend every Saturday under the hood of that thing. He'd always have a cigar in his mouth, even when he was leaning right over the fuel line. Looking back, that probably wasn't the safest thing to do, was it? But he never blew us up. He was a tough old bird, my uncle. Worked at the steel mill for forty years until his back gave out. He used to tell the best stories about the guys at the mill. There was this one guy they called 'Lefty' because... well, you can probably guess. Lost two fingers in a stamping press. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, my thoughts making sense. Yeah, no, I think just like anybody else. I don't have any weird thoughts or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others around you didn’t seem to understand or agree with, even though you felt certain it was true?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that others didn't? You mean like... like a conspiracy theory or something? No, I don't get into any of that nonsense. People get so worked up over things they read on the internet. My sister, she's always sending me these videos about how they're putting things in the water supply. I tell her, \"Linda, the only thing in the water is too much chlorine.\" You can smell it when you turn on the tap sometimes. It reminds me of the public pool we used to go to when we were kids. The one over on Elm Street? It's a parking lot now, of course. But back then, they'd dump so much bleach in there your eyes would sting for two days after. We'd stay in the water until our fingers looked like raisins. We'd ride our bikes there, no helmets, no parents watching us. Just gone all day until the streetlights came on. It's a wonder any of us survived, honestly. Kids today, they don't even go outside unless it's an organized sport with uniforms and a schedule. It's sad, really. They're missing out on just... being a kid. But, uh, what did you ask me? Oh, believing things other people don't. No, no. I just believe what's right in front of me. Common sense, mostly. Nothing unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused on tasks or activities at home, like reading the newspaper, watching TV, or following a conversation?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I suppose so. Like, I can sit down with a set of notes or, you know, a gallery packet, and I'll start out just fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes keep moving over the page while my mind has completely wandered off, and I realize I haven't absorbed a single word and I have to read the exact same part all over again. It's frustrating. \n\nMy mind just goes elsewhere, usually thinking about something I need to fix around the house. Like the gutters. They've been backed up since that big storm we had last month. The wind was howling so loud it actually knocked down the old oak tree in the Henderson's yard next door. Missed their roof by absolute inches! The city had to come out with those massive chainsaws to cut it up and haul it away. Took them three whole days. The noise was unbelievable, just this loud buzzing from sunup to sundown. I actually went out and talked to one of the crew guys, a young kid, maybe twenty. Offered him a bottle of water because it was sweltering out there. He told me he was saving up to buy a motorcycle. A Harley, I think he said. I had a motorcycle when I was his age, an old Triumph. God, I loved that bike. I rode it all the way up the coast one summer with a buddy of mine. We slept in a tent that leaked every time it rained, which was... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about the newspaper?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, or that you don’t look forward to activities the way you once did?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I still enjoy things just fine. I still look forward to my usual routine. I mean, I like sitting out on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning. Watching the neighborhood wake up. The paperboy still comes around 6 AM, though he doesn't throw it from a bicycle anymore. He drives a little sedan and just sort of tosses it out the window. It's not the same. When I was a kid, delivering papers was a real job. You had the bag over your shoulder, riding your Schwinn up and down the hills. I had the biggest route in my neighborhood. Made enough money to buy my first baseball glove. A Rawlings, genuine leather. The smell of that leather when it was brand new... nothing like it. I used to rub it down with neat's-foot oil and stick a baseball in it, wrap it tight with rubber bands, and put it under my mattress to break it in. That's how you got the perfect pocket. We played pickup games in the empty lot behind the hardware store until it got too dark to see the ball. We didn't have umpires or coaches, we just figured it out ourselves. If there was an argument over a call, we'd just do a do-over. Simple as that. The hardware store owner, Mr. Higgins, he'd come out sometimes and watch us. He had this big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. He sold me my first set of socket wrenches when I was sixteen. I still have them, actually. Quality tools last a lifetime, not like the cheap stuff you buy at the big box stores today. You buy a wrench now, it strips the first time you use it. It's a shame. \n\nBut, uh, enjoying things? Yeah, I still enjoy my coffee and my tools. I'm doing alright.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like working at the museum or fixing things around the house, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I wouldn't say I've noticed any changes like that. I still like puttering around the house, fixing things. I mean, there's always something that needs fixing, right? Just last week, the hinge on the screen door started squeaking. It's one of those old wooden doors, the kind that slams if you don't catch it. My wife used to yell at me about letting it slam. \"You're going to break the glass!\" she'd say. We bought that door at a salvage yard back in '98. Or maybe it was '99? We had just gotten that golden retriever puppy, Buster. Oh, Buster was a handful. He chewed through the leg of the dining room table his first week with us. Solid mahogany, too. My father-in-law was furious, he gave us that table as a wedding gift. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses in the new subdivision on the east side of town. Back when they actually used real wood, not this particle board nonsense they throw together now. You lean on a wall in a new house today, you might just go right through it. It's all about doing it fast and cheap now. Nobody takes pride in their craftsmanship anymore. My father-in-law, he could run his hand over a piece of sanded oak and tell you exactly what grit sandpaper you used. He had these thick, calloused hands. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, the museum and fixing things. No, no, I still enjoy all that just the same. Nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in the things you used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get a kick out of the same things I always have. Like my morning coffee, or just being out in the garage. I was out there yesterday, actually, reorganizing my socket set. I have this old Craftsman set, the kind they made back in the seventies when the steel was actually good. My uncle gave it to me when I graduated high school. He worked at the Sears auto center for thirty-something years. Wore that blue uniform with his name stitched over the pocket—'Ed'. He was a big guy, had hands like baseball mitts. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? He'd always have a cigar in his mouth, even when he was leaning right over the fuel line. Looking back, that probably wasn't the safest thing to do, was it? But he never blew us up. He was a tough old bird, my uncle. Worked at the steel mill for forty years until his back gave out. He used to tell the best stories about the guys at the mill. There was this one guy they called 'Lefty' because... well, you can probably guess. Lost two fingers in a stamping press. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, my thoughts making sense. Yeah, no, I think just like anybody else. I don't have any weird thoughts or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others around you didn’t seem to understand or agree with, even though you felt certain it was true?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that others didn't? You mean like... like a conspiracy theory or something? No, I don't get into any of that nonsense. People get so worked up over things they read on the internet. My sister, she's always sending me these videos about how they're putting things in the water supply. I tell her, \"Linda, the only thing in the water is too much chlorine.\" You can smell it when you turn on the tap sometimes. It reminds me of the public pool we used to go to when we were kids. The one over on Elm Street? It's a parking lot now, of course. But back then, they'd dump so much bleach in there your eyes would sting for two days after. We'd stay in the water until our fingers looked like raisins. We'd ride our bikes there, no helmets, no parents watching us. Just gone all day until the streetlights came on. It's a wonder any of us survived, honestly. Kids today, they don't even go outside unless it's an organized sport with uniforms and a schedule. It's sad, really. They're missing out on just... being a kid. But, uh, what did you ask me? Oh, believing things other people don't. No, no. I just believe what's right in front of me. Common sense, mostly. Nothing unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused on tasks or activities at home, like reading the newspaper, watching TV, or following a conversation?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I suppose so. Like, I can sit down with a set of notes or, you know, a gallery packet, and I'll start out just fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes keep moving over the page while my mind has completely wandered off, and I realize I haven't absorbed a single word and I have to read the exact same part all over again. It's frustrating. \n\nMy mind just goes elsewhere, usually thinking about something I need to fix around the house. Like the gutters. They've been backed up since that big storm we had last month. The wind was howling so loud it actually knocked down the old oak tree in the Henderson's yard next door. Missed their roof by absolute inches! The city had to come out with those massive chainsaws to cut it up and haul it away. Took them three whole days. The noise was unbelievable, just this loud buzzing from sunup to sundown. I actually went out and talked to one of the crew guys, a young kid, maybe twenty. Offered him a bottle of water because it was sweltering out there. He told me he was saving up to buy a motorcycle. A Harley, I think he said. I had a motorcycle when I was his age, an old Triumph. God, I loved that bike. I rode it all the way up the coast one summer with a buddy of mine. We slept in a tent that leaked every time it rained, which was... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about the newspaper?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, or that you don’t look forward to activities the way you once did?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I still enjoy things just fine. I still look forward to my usual routine. I mean, I like sitting out on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning. Watching the neighborhood wake up. The paperboy still comes around 6 AM, though he doesn't throw it from a bicycle anymore. He drives a little sedan and just sort of tosses it out the window. It's not the same. When I was a kid, delivering papers was a real job. You had the bag over your shoulder, riding your Schwinn up and down the hills. I had the biggest route in my neighborhood. Made enough money to buy my first baseball glove. A Rawlings, genuine leather. The smell of that leather when it was brand new... nothing like it. I used to rub it down with neat's-foot oil and stick a baseball in it, wrap it tight with rubber bands, and put it under my mattress to break it in. That's how you got the perfect pocket. We played pickup games in the empty lot behind the hardware store until it got too dark to see the ball. We didn't have umpires or coaches, we just figured it out ourselves. If there was an argument over a call, we'd just do a do-over. Simple as that. The hardware store owner, Mr. Higgins, he'd come out sometimes and watch us. He had this big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. He sold me my first set of socket wrenches when I was sixteen. I still have them, actually. Quality tools last a lifetime, not like the cheap stuff you buy at the big box stores today. You buy a wrench now, it strips the first time you use it. It's a shame. \n\nBut, uh, enjoying things? Yeah, I still enjoy my coffee and my tools. I'm doing alright.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like working at the museum or fixing things around the house, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I wouldn't say I've noticed any changes like that. I still like puttering around the house, fixing things. I mean, there's always something that needs fixing, right? Just last week, the hinge on the screen door started squeaking. It's one of those old wooden doors, the kind that slams if you don't catch it. My wife used to yell at me about letting it slam. \"You're going to break the glass!\" she'd say. We bought that door at a salvage yard back in '98. Or maybe it was '99? We had just gotten that golden retriever puppy, Buster. Oh, Buster was a handful. He chewed through the leg of the dining room table his first week with us. Solid mahogany, too. My father-in-law was furious, he gave us that table as a wedding gift. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses in the new subdivision on the east side of town. Back when they actually used real wood, not this particle board nonsense they throw together now. You lean on a wall in a new house today, you might just go right through it. It's all about doing it fast and cheap now. Nobody takes pride in their craftsmanship anymore. My father-in-law, he could run his hand over a piece of sanded oak and tell you exactly what grit sandpaper you used. He had these thick, calloused hands. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, the museum and fixing things. No, no, I still enjoy all that just the same. Nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in the things you used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get a kick out of the same things I always have. Like my morning coffee, or just being out in the garage. I was out there yesterday, actually, reorganizing my socket set. I have this old Craftsman set, the kind they made back in the seventies when the steel was actually good. My uncle gave it to me when I graduated high school. He worked at the Sears auto center for thirty-something years. Wore that blue uniform with his name stitched over the pocket—'Ed'. He was a big guy, had hands like baseball mitts. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? I feel like you keep asking me the same thing, Doc. No, I get plenty of satisfaction out of my day. Like I said, I like my routine. Just yesterday I was out back, mowing the lawn. I still use a gas-powered Toro. Bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, must have been 2005. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems. My sister Carol went down there a few years ago, bought a little condo in Boca. She complains about the humidity constantly, but she loves the early bird specials at the diners. She always was a penny pincher, Carol. When we were kids, she used to save her Halloween candy all the way until Easter. Just hide it in a shoebox under her bed. I'd eat all mine in three days flat and end up with a terrible stomach ache. Mom used to make us drink ginger ale when we were sick. Real ginger ale, you know, in the heavy green glass bottles. You'd pop the metal cap off and the carbonation would fizz right up your nose. They don't make soda like that anymore, it's all plastic bottles and high fructose corn syrup now. Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? Meaningful? Doc, I gotta be honest, I feel like we're going in circles here. I mean, yes, I still enjoy the museum and my tools. Just the other day, I was down at the museum, dusting off the display cases in the geology exhibit. They have this one piece of amethyst, must be the size of a bowling ball. Beautiful purple color. It reminds me of this sweater my wife used to wear back when we were first dating. We went to the state fair one year, she was wearing that sweater, and we rode the Ferris wheel. It was one of those rickety old ones that creaked every time it stopped. I hate heights, always have. My stomach was doing flip-flops the whole time, but I didn't want to look like a coward in front of her. We ended up getting cotton candy after, the blue kind that turns your tongue a different color for three days. You know, they charge an arm and a leg for a bag of cotton candy now. Five dollars! For spun sugar and air. It's highway robbery. Everything's like that now, though. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and you're out twenty bucks. It's hard for folks on a fixed income to keep up. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The museum? Yeah, no, I still find it all rewarding. I don't feel like anything's changed with that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Less interested? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say it. I'm just as interested as I ever was. Engaged, interested, satisfied, whatever word you want to use. I still go to the museum every Tuesday and Thursday. In fact, I was just talking to the curator, Mr. Henderson. Nice fellow, but he wears these bowties that are always just a little bit crooked. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi there, Dr. [Your Name]. Um, what brings me in... well, it's this thing that keeps happening down at the museum. I volunteer there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, mostly helping with the local history exhibits. They've got this incredible collection of 19th-century farm equipment right now, actually. You wouldn't believe how heavy those old plows are. But anyway, people at the museum will be talking right to me, like about the schedule for the next art walk or something, and I'll be looking right at them, nodding, you know? But then I realize I completely missed the middle part of what they just said. My mind just sort of went somewhere else, maybe thinking about the lighting in the east gallery, and I have to ask them to run it by me all over again. It's getting a bit embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of lapse in attention happen in other situations, like when you're talking with friends or family, or is it mostly just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Well, with my friends or family... um, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it happening with them. Like, I see my brother Tom every Sunday for breakfast. We always go down to that diner over on Main Street—the one with the broken neon sign? They used to make this incredible cherry pie, just like our mom used to make, but they changed the recipe a couple of years ago. I actually asked the waitress about it, and she said they switched to some cheaper canned filling to save money. It's a real shame, honestly, because you just can't find a good, proper fruit pie around here anymore. Bakeries just don't do it the old way. But, um... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, listening to people. Yeah, I don't know, Tom talks my ear off about his tomato plants every week, and I think I catch most of what he's saying. So I guess it's mostly just a museum thing, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory lately, like forgetting things more often, getting lost in familiar places, or having trouble doing things you used to do easily?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, no, my memory's sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember the exact layout of the old hardware store before it burned down in '84. You know, the one over by the tracks. They had those wooden floors that creaked every time you stepped on them, and the smell... it was this mix of sawdust and motor oil. I used to go in there with my dad to buy nails by the pound. You don't see that anymore, do you? Everything's in those little plastic blister packs now. You buy a pack of screws, and you only need three, but you have to buy twenty, and then the rest just sit in a jar in the garage for a decade. I've got a whole coffee can full of them right now. An old Folgers can. But anyway, getting lost? No, no, I know my way around town just fine. I haven't noticed any problems like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get out of bed just fine. I still like going to the museum, like I said. Tuesday and Thursday mornings, regular as clockwork. I usually take the bus down there, the number 4. It's a nice ride, usually pretty quiet that time of day. Though last week, there was this woman on there with a parrot. A real, live parrot in a cage. Just sitting on her lap. It kept making this sound like a cell phone ringing, and half the people on the bus kept checking their pockets. I had to laugh. It's funny what people bring on public transit these days, isn't it? Back when I was working at the plant—before they closed it down in '08, you know—I used to drive my old Ford truck. Loved that truck. Two-tone blue and white. But gas got so expensive, and honestly, the bus is just easier now. No parking to worry about. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, no, I feel fine. Just regular, I guess. Nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you saw, heard, or felt things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: See or hear things? Like... like ghosts or something? No, no, I've never had anything like that happen. My mind doesn't play tricks on me. Though, you know, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins, she swears her house is haunted. Says she hears footsteps in the attic every night around 2 AM. I told her it's probably just raccoons. We get a lot of them around here, especially since they tore down that patch of woods behind the old elementary school to build those new townhouses. Ugly things, too, all gray vinyl siding. No character at all. They put them up so fast, I bet the plumbing is a nightmare. I remember when my buddy Stan bought a new build back in the nineties, and the pipes burst his first winter. Ruined his whole basement. He had this beautiful model train setup down there, O-gauge, took him years to build the little towns and mountains. Just washed it all away. It was a real shame. Stan was so heartbroken he never rebuilt it. He took up birdwatching instead. But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. No, I don't hear or see anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that other people don’t seem to understand, or felt like your thoughts aren’t making sense in a way that’s different from how other people think?\n\nPatient: Thoughts that don't make sense? No, my thoughts are perfectly clear to me. I mean, sometimes I'll try to explain how to fix a carburetor to my nephew, and he just stares at me blankly, but that's because kids these days don't know the first thing about engines. It's all computers now. You plug a little machine into the dashboard and it tells you what's wrong. Where's the skill in that? I learned by listening to the engine, you know? My uncle taught me. He had this old Chevy, a '57 Bel Air, cherry red. Beautiful car. We used to spend every Saturday under the hood of that thing. He'd always have a cigar in his mouth, even when he was leaning right over the fuel line. Looking back, that probably wasn't the safest thing to do, was it? But he never blew us up. He was a tough old bird, my uncle. Worked at the steel mill for forty years until his back gave out. He used to tell the best stories about the guys at the mill. There was this one guy they called 'Lefty' because... well, you can probably guess. Lost two fingers in a stamping press. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, my thoughts making sense. Yeah, no, I think just like anybody else. I don't have any weird thoughts or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others around you didn’t seem to understand or agree with, even though you felt certain it was true?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that others didn't? You mean like... like a conspiracy theory or something? No, I don't get into any of that nonsense. People get so worked up over things they read on the internet. My sister, she's always sending me these videos about how they're putting things in the water supply. I tell her, \"Linda, the only thing in the water is too much chlorine.\" You can smell it when you turn on the tap sometimes. It reminds me of the public pool we used to go to when we were kids. The one over on Elm Street? It's a parking lot now, of course. But back then, they'd dump so much bleach in there your eyes would sting for two days after. We'd stay in the water until our fingers looked like raisins. We'd ride our bikes there, no helmets, no parents watching us. Just gone all day until the streetlights came on. It's a wonder any of us survived, honestly. Kids today, they don't even go outside unless it's an organized sport with uniforms and a schedule. It's sad, really. They're missing out on just... being a kid. But, uh, what did you ask me? Oh, believing things other people don't. No, no. I just believe what's right in front of me. Common sense, mostly. Nothing unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused on tasks or activities at home, like reading the newspaper, watching TV, or following a conversation?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I suppose so. Like, I can sit down with a set of notes or, you know, a gallery packet, and I'll start out just fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes keep moving over the page while my mind has completely wandered off, and I realize I haven't absorbed a single word and I have to read the exact same part all over again. It's frustrating. \n\nMy mind just goes elsewhere, usually thinking about something I need to fix around the house. Like the gutters. They've been backed up since that big storm we had last month. The wind was howling so loud it actually knocked down the old oak tree in the Henderson's yard next door. Missed their roof by absolute inches! The city had to come out with those massive chainsaws to cut it up and haul it away. Took them three whole days. The noise was unbelievable, just this loud buzzing from sunup to sundown. I actually went out and talked to one of the crew guys, a young kid, maybe twenty. Offered him a bottle of water because it was sweltering out there. He told me he was saving up to buy a motorcycle. A Harley, I think he said. I had a motorcycle when I was his age, an old Triumph. God, I loved that bike. I rode it all the way up the coast one summer with a buddy of mine. We slept in a tent that leaked every time it rained, which was... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about the newspaper?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, or that you don’t look forward to activities the way you once did?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I still enjoy things just fine. I still look forward to my usual routine. I mean, I like sitting out on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning. Watching the neighborhood wake up. The paperboy still comes around 6 AM, though he doesn't throw it from a bicycle anymore. He drives a little sedan and just sort of tosses it out the window. It's not the same. When I was a kid, delivering papers was a real job. You had the bag over your shoulder, riding your Schwinn up and down the hills. I had the biggest route in my neighborhood. Made enough money to buy my first baseball glove. A Rawlings, genuine leather. The smell of that leather when it was brand new... nothing like it. I used to rub it down with neat's-foot oil and stick a baseball in it, wrap it tight with rubber bands, and put it under my mattress to break it in. That's how you got the perfect pocket. We played pickup games in the empty lot behind the hardware store until it got too dark to see the ball. We didn't have umpires or coaches, we just figured it out ourselves. If there was an argument over a call, we'd just do a do-over. Simple as that. The hardware store owner, Mr. Higgins, he'd come out sometimes and watch us. He had this big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. He sold me my first set of socket wrenches when I was sixteen. I still have them, actually. Quality tools last a lifetime, not like the cheap stuff you buy at the big box stores today. You buy a wrench now, it strips the first time you use it. It's a shame. \n\nBut, uh, enjoying things? Yeah, I still enjoy my coffee and my tools. I'm doing alright.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like working at the museum or fixing things around the house, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I wouldn't say I've noticed any changes like that. I still like puttering around the house, fixing things. I mean, there's always something that needs fixing, right? Just last week, the hinge on the screen door started squeaking. It's one of those old wooden doors, the kind that slams if you don't catch it. My wife used to yell at me about letting it slam. \"You're going to break the glass!\" she'd say. We bought that door at a salvage yard back in '98. Or maybe it was '99? We had just gotten that golden retriever puppy, Buster. Oh, Buster was a handful. He chewed through the leg of the dining room table his first week with us. Solid mahogany, too. My father-in-law was furious, he gave us that table as a wedding gift. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses in the new subdivision on the east side of town. Back when they actually used real wood, not this particle board nonsense they throw together now. You lean on a wall in a new house today, you might just go right through it. It's all about doing it fast and cheap now. Nobody takes pride in their craftsmanship anymore. My father-in-law, he could run his hand over a piece of sanded oak and tell you exactly what grit sandpaper you used. He had these thick, calloused hands. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, the museum and fixing things. No, no, I still enjoy all that just the same. Nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in the things you used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get a kick out of the same things I always have. Like my morning coffee, or just being out in the garage. I was out there yesterday, actually, reorganizing my socket set. I have this old Craftsman set, the kind they made back in the seventies when the steel was actually good. My uncle gave it to me when I graduated high school. He worked at the Sears auto center for thirty-something years. Wore that blue uniform with his name stitched over the pocket—'Ed'. He was a big guy, had hands like baseball mitts. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? I feel like you keep asking me the same thing, Doc. No, I get plenty of satisfaction out of my day. Like I said, I like my routine. Just yesterday I was out back, mowing the lawn. I still use a gas-powered Toro. Bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, must have been 2005. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems. My sister Carol went down there a few years ago, bought a little condo in Boca. She complains about the humidity constantly, but she loves the early bird specials at the diners. She always was a penny pincher, Carol. When we were kids, she used to save her Halloween candy all the way until Easter. Just hide it in a shoebox under her bed. I'd eat all mine in three days flat and end up with a terrible stomach ache. Mom used to make us drink ginger ale when we were sick. Real ginger ale, you know, in the heavy green glass bottles. You'd pop the metal cap off and the carbonation would fizz right up your nose. They don't make soda like that anymore, it's all plastic bottles and high fructose corn syrup now. Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? Meaningful? Doc, I gotta be honest, I feel like we're going in circles here. I mean, yes, I still enjoy the museum and my tools. Just the other day, I was down at the museum, dusting off the display cases in the geology exhibit. They have this one piece of amethyst, must be the size of a bowling ball. Beautiful purple color. It reminds me of this sweater my wife used to wear back when we were first dating. We went to the state fair one year, she was wearing that sweater, and we rode the Ferris wheel. It was one of those rickety old ones that creaked every time it stopped. I hate heights, always have. My stomach was doing flip-flops the whole time, but I didn't want to look like a coward in front of her. We ended up getting cotton candy after, the blue kind that turns your tongue a different color for three days. You know, they charge an arm and a leg for a bag of cotton candy now. Five dollars! For spun sugar and air. It's highway robbery. Everything's like that now, though. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and you're out twenty bucks. It's hard for folks on a fixed income to keep up. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The museum? Yeah, no, I still find it all rewarding. I don't feel like anything's changed with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say it. I'm just as interested as I ever was. Engaged, interested, satisfied, whatever word you want to use. I still go to the museum every Tuesday and Thursday. In fact, I was just talking to the curator, Mr. Henderson. Nice fellow, but he wears these bowties that are always just a little bit crooked. It drives me crazy, but I don't have the heart to tell him. He's got this old beagle named Buster that he brings into the office sometimes. Buster's mostly blind now, poor thing, just bumps into the filing cabinets. We had a beagle when I was a boy. Snoopy, we called him. Original, I know. But he was a great dog. Used to chase the mail truck all the way down Elm Street every single morning. The mailman, Mr. Peterson, would actually save dog biscuits in his pocket just for him. You don't see mailmen doing that anymore. Now they just drive up in those little white trucks and shove the mail in the box and speed off. Half the time they get my mail mixed up with the guy across the street. He gets my electric bill, I get his car magazines. He's got a classic Mustang he's restoring. Beautiful car, baby blue. \n\nAnyway... no, I'm still engaged. Everything's exactly the same. I don't know why we keep going over this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

He had this big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. He sold me my first set of socket wrenches when I was sixteen. I still have them, actually. Quality tools last a lifetime, not like the cheap stuff you buy at the big box stores today. You buy a wrench now, it strips the first time you use it. It's a shame. \n\nBut, uh, enjoying things? Yeah, I still enjoy my coffee and my tools. I'm doing alright.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like working at the museum or fixing things around the house, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I wouldn't say I've noticed any changes like that. I still like puttering around the house, fixing things. I mean, there's always something that needs fixing, right? Just last week, the hinge on the screen door started squeaking. It's one of those old wooden doors, the kind that slams if you don't catch it. My wife used to yell at me about letting it slam. \"You're going to break the glass!\" she'd say. We bought that door at a salvage yard back in '98. Or maybe it was '99? We had just gotten that golden retriever puppy, Buster. Oh, Buster was a handful. He chewed through the leg of the dining room table his first week with us. Solid mahogany, too. My father-in-law was furious, he gave us that table as a wedding gift. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses in the new subdivision on the east side of town. Back when they actually used real wood, not this particle board nonsense they throw together now. You lean on a wall in a new house today, you might just go right through it. It's all about doing it fast and cheap now. Nobody takes pride in their craftsmanship anymore. My father-in-law, he could run his hand over a piece of sanded oak and tell you exactly what grit sandpaper you used. He had these thick, calloused hands. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, the museum and fixing things. No, no, I still enjoy all that just the same. Nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in the things you used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get a kick out of the same things I always have. Like my morning coffee, or just being out in the garage. I was out there yesterday, actually, reorganizing my socket set. I have this old Craftsman set, the kind they made back in the seventies when the steel was actually good. My uncle gave it to me when I graduated high school. He worked at the Sears auto center for thirty-something years. Wore that blue uniform with his name stitched over the pocket—'Ed'. He was a big guy, had hands like baseball mitts. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? I feel like you keep asking me the same thing, Doc. No, I get plenty of satisfaction out of my day. Like I said, I like my routine. Just yesterday I was out back, mowing the lawn. I still use a gas-powered Toro. Bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, must have been 2005. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems. My sister Carol went down there a few years ago, bought a little condo in Boca. She complains about the humidity constantly, but she loves the early bird specials at the diners. She always was a penny pincher, Carol. When we were kids, she used to save her Halloween candy all the way until Easter. Just hide it in a shoebox under her bed. I'd eat all mine in three days flat and end up with a terrible stomach ache. Mom used to make us drink ginger ale when we were sick. Real ginger ale, you know, in the heavy green glass bottles. You'd pop the metal cap off and the carbonation would fizz right up your nose. They don't make soda like that anymore, it's all plastic bottles and high fructose corn syrup now. Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? Meaningful? Doc, I gotta be honest, I feel like we're going in circles here. I mean, yes, I still enjoy the museum and my tools. Just the other day, I was down at the museum, dusting off the display cases in the geology exhibit. They have this one piece of amethyst, must be the size of a bowling ball. Beautiful purple color. It reminds me of this sweater my wife used to wear back when we were first dating. We went to the state fair one year, she was wearing that sweater, and we rode the Ferris wheel. It was one of those rickety old ones that creaked every time it stopped. I hate heights, always have. My stomach was doing flip-flops the whole time, but I didn't want to look like a coward in front of her. We ended up getting cotton candy after, the blue kind that turns your tongue a different color for three days. You know, they charge an arm and a leg for a bag of cotton candy now. Five dollars! For spun sugar and air. It's highway robbery. Everything's like that now, though. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and you're out twenty bucks. It's hard for folks on a fixed income to keep up. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The museum? Yeah, no, I still find it all rewarding. I don't feel like anything's changed with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say it. I'm just as interested as I ever was. Engaged, interested, satisfied, whatever word you want to use. I still go to the museum every Tuesday and Thursday. In fact, I was just talking to the curator, Mr. Henderson. Nice fellow, but he wears these bowties that are always just a little bit crooked. It drives me crazy, but I don't have the heart to tell him. He's got this old beagle named Buster that he brings into the office sometimes. Buster's mostly blind now, poor thing, just bumps into the filing cabinets. We had a beagle when I was a boy. Snoopy, we called him. Original, I know. But he was a great dog. Used to chase the mail truck all the way down Elm Street every single morning. The mailman, Mr. Peterson, would actually save dog biscuits in his pocket just for him. You don't see mailmen doing that anymore. Now they just drive up in those little white trucks and shove the mail in the box and speed off. Half the time they get my mail mixed up with the guy across the street. He gets my electric bill, I get his car magazines. He's got a classic Mustang he's restoring. Beautiful car, baby blue. \n\nAnyway... no, I'm still engaged. Everything's exactly the same. I don't know why we keep going over this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doc, are you feeling alright? You literally just asked me that exact same question. Less interested, less engaged... no, my answer is still no. It's like a broken record in here today. 

Speaking of records, my buddy Sal, he used to collect vinyl. Had thousands of them down in his basement. The weight of all those boxes actually started bowing the floorboards above. His wife, Marie, she was absolutely furious. She collected those little porcelain figurines... Precious Moments, I think they were called? Little kids with giant teardrop eyes. Creepy looking things, if you ask me. She had them lined up all over the living room shelves. You couldn't even dust without knocking one over and chipping it. I knocked over a little shepherd boy once, tried to glue the crook back on with some epoxy I had in the garage, but it never looked right again. She never let me hear the end of it. She held a grudge like nobody's business. 

Anyway, Sal's floorboards... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, your question. No, I'm just as engaged in my projects as always. Honestly, Doc, I don't know why we're stuck on this. Everything is fine.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi there, Dr. [Your Name]. Um, what brings me in... well, it's this thing that keeps happening down at the museum. I volunteer there on Tuesdays and Thursdays, mostly helping with the local history exhibits. They've got this incredible collection of 19th-century farm equipment right now, actually. You wouldn't believe how heavy those old plows are. But anyway, people at the museum will be talking right to me, like about the schedule for the next art walk or something, and I'll be looking right at them, nodding, you know? But then I realize I completely missed the middle part of what they just said. My mind just sort of went somewhere else, maybe thinking about the lighting in the east gallery, and I have to ask them to run it by me all over again. It's getting a bit embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of lapse in attention happen in other situations, like when you're talking with friends or family, or is it mostly just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Well, with my friends or family... um, I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed it happening with them. Like, I see my brother Tom every Sunday for breakfast. We always go down to that diner over on Main Street—the one with the broken neon sign? They used to make this incredible cherry pie, just like our mom used to make, but they changed the recipe a couple of years ago. I actually asked the waitress about it, and she said they switched to some cheaper canned filling to save money. It's a real shame, honestly, because you just can't find a good, proper fruit pie around here anymore. Bakeries just don't do it the old way. But, um... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, listening to people. Yeah, I don't know, Tom talks my ear off about his tomato plants every week, and I think I catch most of what he's saying. So I guess it's mostly just a museum thing, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory lately, like forgetting things more often, getting lost in familiar places, or having trouble doing things you used to do easily?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, no, my memory's sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember the exact layout of the old hardware store before it burned down in '84. You know, the one over by the tracks. They had those wooden floors that creaked every time you stepped on them, and the smell... it was this mix of sawdust and motor oil. I used to go in there with my dad to buy nails by the pound. You don't see that anymore, do you? Everything's in those little plastic blister packs now. You buy a pack of screws, and you only need three, but you have to buy twenty, and then the rest just sit in a jar in the garage for a decade. I've got a whole coffee can full of them right now. An old Folgers can. But anyway, getting lost? No, no, I know my way around town just fine. I haven't noticed any problems like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Feeling down? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get out of bed just fine. I still like going to the museum, like I said. Tuesday and Thursday mornings, regular as clockwork. I usually take the bus down there, the number 4. It's a nice ride, usually pretty quiet that time of day. Though last week, there was this woman on there with a parrot. A real, live parrot in a cage. Just sitting on her lap. It kept making this sound like a cell phone ringing, and half the people on the bus kept checking their pockets. I had to laugh. It's funny what people bring on public transit these days, isn't it? Back when I was working at the plant—before they closed it down in '08, you know—I used to drive my old Ford truck. Loved that truck. Two-tone blue and white. But gas got so expensive, and honestly, the bus is just easier now. No parking to worry about. But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling sad. No, no, I feel fine. Just regular, I guess. Nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you saw, heard, or felt things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: See or hear things? Like... like ghosts or something? No, no, I've never had anything like that happen. My mind doesn't play tricks on me. Though, you know, my neighbor Mrs. Higgins, she swears her house is haunted. Says she hears footsteps in the attic every night around 2 AM. I told her it's probably just raccoons. We get a lot of them around here, especially since they tore down that patch of woods behind the old elementary school to build those new townhouses. Ugly things, too, all gray vinyl siding. No character at all. They put them up so fast, I bet the plumbing is a nightmare. I remember when my buddy Stan bought a new build back in the nineties, and the pipes burst his first winter. Ruined his whole basement. He had this beautiful model train setup down there, O-gauge, took him years to build the little towns and mountains. Just washed it all away. It was a real shame. Stan was so heartbroken he never rebuilt it. He took up birdwatching instead. But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, hearing things. No, I don't hear or see anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts that other people don’t seem to understand, or felt like your thoughts aren’t making sense in a way that’s different from how other people think?\n\nPatient: Thoughts that don't make sense? No, my thoughts are perfectly clear to me. I mean, sometimes I'll try to explain how to fix a carburetor to my nephew, and he just stares at me blankly, but that's because kids these days don't know the first thing about engines. It's all computers now. You plug a little machine into the dashboard and it tells you what's wrong. Where's the skill in that? I learned by listening to the engine, you know? My uncle taught me. He had this old Chevy, a '57 Bel Air, cherry red. Beautiful car. We used to spend every Saturday under the hood of that thing. He'd always have a cigar in his mouth, even when he was leaning right over the fuel line. Looking back, that probably wasn't the safest thing to do, was it? But he never blew us up. He was a tough old bird, my uncle. Worked at the steel mill for forty years until his back gave out. He used to tell the best stories about the guys at the mill. There was this one guy they called 'Lefty' because... well, you can probably guess. Lost two fingers in a stamping press. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, my thoughts making sense. Yeah, no, I think just like anybody else. I don't have any weird thoughts or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that others around you didn’t seem to understand or agree with, even though you felt certain it was true?\n\nPatient: Believed something strongly that others didn't? You mean like... like a conspiracy theory or something? No, I don't get into any of that nonsense. People get so worked up over things they read on the internet. My sister, she's always sending me these videos about how they're putting things in the water supply. I tell her, \"Linda, the only thing in the water is too much chlorine.\" You can smell it when you turn on the tap sometimes. It reminds me of the public pool we used to go to when we were kids. The one over on Elm Street? It's a parking lot now, of course. But back then, they'd dump so much bleach in there your eyes would sting for two days after. We'd stay in the water until our fingers looked like raisins. We'd ride our bikes there, no helmets, no parents watching us. Just gone all day until the streetlights came on. It's a wonder any of us survived, honestly. Kids today, they don't even go outside unless it's an organized sport with uniforms and a schedule. It's sad, really. They're missing out on just... being a kid. But, uh, what did you ask me? Oh, believing things other people don't. No, no. I just believe what's right in front of me. Common sense, mostly. Nothing unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused on tasks or activities at home, like reading the newspaper, watching TV, or following a conversation?\n\nPatient: Focusing? Yeah, I suppose so. Like, I can sit down with a set of notes or, you know, a gallery packet, and I'll start out just fine. But after a few minutes, my eyes keep moving over the page while my mind has completely wandered off, and I realize I haven't absorbed a single word and I have to read the exact same part all over again. It's frustrating. \n\nMy mind just goes elsewhere, usually thinking about something I need to fix around the house. Like the gutters. They've been backed up since that big storm we had last month. The wind was howling so loud it actually knocked down the old oak tree in the Henderson's yard next door. Missed their roof by absolute inches! The city had to come out with those massive chainsaws to cut it up and haul it away. Took them three whole days. The noise was unbelievable, just this loud buzzing from sunup to sundown. I actually went out and talked to one of the crew guys, a young kid, maybe twenty. Offered him a bottle of water because it was sweltering out there. He told me he was saving up to buy a motorcycle. A Harley, I think he said. I had a motorcycle when I was his age, an old Triumph. God, I loved that bike. I rode it all the way up the coast one summer with a buddy of mine. We slept in a tent that leaked every time it rained, which was... \n\nWait, what was the question? Something about the newspaper?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, or that you don’t look forward to activities the way you once did?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I still enjoy things just fine. I still look forward to my usual routine. I mean, I like sitting out on the porch with a cup of coffee in the morning. Watching the neighborhood wake up. The paperboy still comes around 6 AM, though he doesn't throw it from a bicycle anymore. He drives a little sedan and just sort of tosses it out the window. It's not the same. When I was a kid, delivering papers was a real job. You had the bag over your shoulder, riding your Schwinn up and down the hills. I had the biggest route in my neighborhood. Made enough money to buy my first baseball glove. A Rawlings, genuine leather. The smell of that leather when it was brand new... nothing like it. I used to rub it down with neat's-foot oil and stick a baseball in it, wrap it tight with rubber bands, and put it under my mattress to break it in. That's how you got the perfect pocket. We played pickup games in the empty lot behind the hardware store until it got too dark to see the ball. We didn't have umpires or coaches, we just figured it out ourselves. If there was an argument over a call, we'd just do a do-over. Simple as that. The hardware store owner, Mr. Higgins, he'd come out sometimes and watch us. He had this big bushy mustache that covered his whole mouth. He sold me my first set of socket wrenches when I was sixteen. I still have them, actually. Quality tools last a lifetime, not like the cheap stuff you buy at the big box stores today. You buy a wrench now, it strips the first time you use it. It's a shame. \n\nBut, uh, enjoying things? Yeah, I still enjoy my coffee and my tools. I'm doing alright.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy your usual activities, like working at the museum or fixing things around the house, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Enjoyment? No, I wouldn't say I've noticed any changes like that. I still like puttering around the house, fixing things. I mean, there's always something that needs fixing, right? Just last week, the hinge on the screen door started squeaking. It's one of those old wooden doors, the kind that slams if you don't catch it. My wife used to yell at me about letting it slam. \"You're going to break the glass!\" she'd say. We bought that door at a salvage yard back in '98. Or maybe it was '99? We had just gotten that golden retriever puppy, Buster. Oh, Buster was a handful. He chewed through the leg of the dining room table his first week with us. Solid mahogany, too. My father-in-law was furious, he gave us that table as a wedding gift. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses in the new subdivision on the east side of town. Back when they actually used real wood, not this particle board nonsense they throw together now. You lean on a wall in a new house today, you might just go right through it. It's all about doing it fast and cheap now. Nobody takes pride in their craftsmanship anymore. My father-in-law, he could run his hand over a piece of sanded oak and tell you exactly what grit sandpaper you used. He had these thick, calloused hands. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, the museum and fixing things. No, no, I still enjoy all that just the same. Nothing's really changed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in the things you used to, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get a kick out of the same things I always have. Like my morning coffee, or just being out in the garage. I was out there yesterday, actually, reorganizing my socket set. I have this old Craftsman set, the kind they made back in the seventies when the steel was actually good. My uncle gave it to me when I graduated high school. He worked at the Sears auto center for thirty-something years. Wore that blue uniform with his name stitched over the pocket—'Ed'. He was a big guy, had hands like baseball mitts. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? I feel like you keep asking me the same thing, Doc. No, I get plenty of satisfaction out of my day. Like I said, I like my routine. Just yesterday I was out back, mowing the lawn. I still use a gas-powered Toro. Bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, must have been 2005. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems. My sister Carol went down there a few years ago, bought a little condo in Boca. She complains about the humidity constantly, but she loves the early bird specials at the diners. She always was a penny pincher, Carol. When we were kids, she used to save her Halloween candy all the way until Easter. Just hide it in a shoebox under her bed. I'd eat all mine in three days flat and end up with a terrible stomach ache. Mom used to make us drink ginger ale when we were sick. Real ginger ale, you know, in the heavy green glass bottles. You'd pop the metal cap off and the carbonation would fizz right up your nose. They don't make soda like that anymore, it's all plastic bottles and high fructose corn syrup now. Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? Meaningful? Doc, I gotta be honest, I feel like we're going in circles here. I mean, yes, I still enjoy the museum and my tools. Just the other day, I was down at the museum, dusting off the display cases in the geology exhibit. They have this one piece of amethyst, must be the size of a bowling ball. Beautiful purple color. It reminds me of this sweater my wife used to wear back when we were first dating. We went to the state fair one year, she was wearing that sweater, and we rode the Ferris wheel. It was one of those rickety old ones that creaked every time it stopped. I hate heights, always have. My stomach was doing flip-flops the whole time, but I didn't want to look like a coward in front of her. We ended up getting cotton candy after, the blue kind that turns your tongue a different color for three days. You know, they charge an arm and a leg for a bag of cotton candy now. Five dollars! For spun sugar and air. It's highway robbery. Everything's like that now, though. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and you're out twenty bucks. It's hard for folks on a fixed income to keep up. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The museum? Yeah, no, I still find it all rewarding. I don't feel like anything's changed with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say it. I'm just as interested as I ever was. Engaged, interested, satisfied, whatever word you want to use. I still go to the museum every Tuesday and Thursday. In fact, I was just talking to the curator, Mr. Henderson. Nice fellow, but he wears these bowties that are always just a little bit crooked. It drives me crazy, but I don't have the heart to tell him. He's got this old beagle named Buster that he brings into the office sometimes. Buster's mostly blind now, poor thing, just bumps into the filing cabinets. We had a beagle when I was a boy. Snoopy, we called him. Original, I know. But he was a great dog. Used to chase the mail truck all the way down Elm Street every single morning. The mailman, Mr. Peterson, would actually save dog biscuits in his pocket just for him. You don't see mailmen doing that anymore. Now they just drive up in those little white trucks and shove the mail in the box and speed off. Half the time they get my mail mixed up with the guy across the street. He gets my electric bill, I get his car magazines. He's got a classic Mustang he's restoring. Beautiful car, baby blue. \n\nAnyway... no, I'm still engaged. Everything's exactly the same. I don't know why we keep going over this.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you feeling alright? You literally just asked me that exact same question. Less interested, less engaged... no, my answer is still no. It's like a broken record in here today. \n\nSpeaking of records, my buddy Sal, he used to collect vinyl. Had thousands of them down in his basement. The weight of all those boxes actually started bowing the floorboards above. His wife, Marie, she was absolutely furious. She collected those little porcelain figurines... Precious Moments, I think they were called? Little kids with giant teardrop eyes. Creepy looking things, if you ask me. She had them lined up all over the living room shelves. You couldn't even dust without knocking one over and chipping it. I knocked over a little shepherd boy once, tried to glue the crook back on with some epoxy I had in the garage, but it never looked right again. She never let me hear the end of it. She held a grudge like nobody's business. \n\nAnyway, Sal's floorboards... wait, what was I saying? Oh, right, your question. No, I'm just as engaged in my projects as always. Honestly, Doc, I don't know why we're stuck on this. Everything is fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He used to take me fishing down at the reservoir. We'd get up before the sun even thought about rising, load up his old Ford pickup... that truck was a classic. Two-tone, cherry red and white. Had an AM radio that only picked up one station, mostly static and country music. We'd sit out on that boat for hours, not saying a word, just watching the bobbers. You don't see kids doing that anymore. They're all inside on their phones or those video games. My grandson, he was over last weekend, didn't look up from his screen the whole time. I tried to show him how to whittle a little boat out of a piece of pine, but he just wasn't interested. It's a shame, really. You miss out on a lot when you're staring at a screen all day. \n\nBut, uh, what was it you were asking? About pleasure? No, I still like doing my usual things. No change there.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still doing your usual activities, have you noticed that you don’t get the same satisfaction or sense of enjoyment out of them as you used to?\n\nPatient: Satisfaction? I feel like you keep asking me the same thing, Doc. No, I get plenty of satisfaction out of my day. Like I said, I like my routine. Just yesterday I was out back, mowing the lawn. I still use a gas-powered Toro. Bought it at a garage sale back in, oh, must have been 2005. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida. Everybody moves to Florida eventually, it seems. My sister Carol went down there a few years ago, bought a little condo in Boca. She complains about the humidity constantly, but she loves the early bird specials at the diners. She always was a penny pincher, Carol. When we were kids, she used to save her Halloween candy all the way until Easter. Just hide it in a shoebox under her bed. I'd eat all mine in three days flat and end up with a terrible stomach ache. Mom used to make us drink ginger ale when we were sick. Real ginger ale, you know, in the heavy green glass bottles. You'd pop the metal cap off and the carbonation would fizz right up your nose. They don't make soda like that anymore, it's all plastic bottles and high fructose corn syrup now. Doesn't taste the same at all. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... satisfaction. Right. No, I'm satisfied. Everything is fine in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like working on tools or spending time at the museum, feel less rewarding or meaningful to you lately, even if you still do them?\n\nPatient: Rewarding? Meaningful? Doc, I gotta be honest, I feel like we're going in circles here. I mean, yes, I still enjoy the museum and my tools. Just the other day, I was down at the museum, dusting off the display cases in the geology exhibit. They have this one piece of amethyst, must be the size of a bowling ball. Beautiful purple color. It reminds me of this sweater my wife used to wear back when we were first dating. We went to the state fair one year, she was wearing that sweater, and we rode the Ferris wheel. It was one of those rickety old ones that creaked every time it stopped. I hate heights, always have. My stomach was doing flip-flops the whole time, but I didn't want to look like a coward in front of her. We ended up getting cotton candy after, the blue kind that turns your tongue a different color for three days. You know, they charge an arm and a leg for a bag of cotton candy now. Five dollars! For spun sugar and air. It's highway robbery. Everything's like that now, though. You go to the grocery store for a loaf of bread and a gallon of milk, and you're out twenty bucks. It's hard for folks on a fixed income to keep up. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? The museum? Yeah, no, I still find it all rewarding. I don't feel like anything's changed with that.\n\nDoctor: Even if you're still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Less interested? Doc, I... I don't know how else to say it. I'm just as interested as I ever was. Engaged, interested, satisfied, whatever word you want to use. I still go to the museum every Tuesday and Thursday. In fact, I was just talking to the curator, Mr. Henderson. Nice fellow, but he wears these bowties that are always just a little bit crooked. It drives me crazy, but I don't have the heart to tell him. He's got this old beagle named Buster that he brings into the office sometimes. Buster's mostly blind now, poor thing, just bumps into the filing cabinets. We had a beagle when I was a boy. Snoopy, we called him. Original, I know. But he was a great dog. Used to chase the mail truck all the way down Elm Street every single morning. The mailman, Mr. Peterson, would actually save dog biscuits in his pocket just for him. You don't see mailmen doing that anymore. Now they just drive up in those little white trucks and shove the mail in the box and speed off. Half the time they get my mail mixed up with the guy across the street. He gets my electric bill, I get his car magazines. He's got a classic Mustang he's restoring. Beautiful car, baby blue. \n\nAnyway... no, I'm still engaged. Everything's exactly the same. I don't know why we keep going over this.\n\nDoctor: Even if you’re still going to the museum and working on projects at home, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less engaged in them than before?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you feeling alright? You literally just asked me that exact same question. Less interested, less engaged... no, my answer is still no. It's like a broken record in here today. \n\nSpeaking of records, my buddy Sal, he used to collect vinyl. Had thousands of them down in his basement. The weight of all those boxes actually started bowing the floorboards above. His wife, Marie, she was absolutely furious. She collected those little porcelain figurines... Precious Moments, I think they were called? Little kids with giant teardrop eyes. Creepy looking things, if you ask me. She had them lined up all over the living room shelves. You couldn't even dust without knocking one over and chipping it. Going through the motions? Doc, I... I swear you're pulling my leg today. Are you reading off a list or something? Because it sounds like the same question in three different suits. 

No, I'm not just going through the motions. I care about the museum, I care about my tools. I was just sharpening my chisels yesterday. You gotta keep a good edge on them, otherwise you're just tearing the wood, not cutting it. I use this Japanese water stone, 8000 grit. Puts a mirror finish on the bevel. My grandfather taught me how to sharpen tools when I was just a boy. He had this old grinding wheel in his shed, the kind you had to pump with a foot pedal. It threw sparks everywhere. He used to smoke a pipe while he worked, cherry tobacco. You could smell it from the driveway. He smoked that pipe right up until he was eighty-five. Never had a cough, never got sick. People were just built tougher back then, I think. Nowadays everyone's allergic to everything. Peanuts, gluten, dairy. My niece's kid can't even eat strawberries without breaking out in hives. How do you go through childhood without eating strawberries? We used to go picking them out at Miller's Farm every June. Your fingers would be stained red for a week. 

But, uh, anyway. No, I'm not just going through the motions. I care about what I do. I really don't know what you're trying to get at here.
